=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and an integer k.
An integer x is almost missing from nums if x appears in exactly one subarray of size k within nums.
Return the largest almost missing integer from nums. If no such integer exists, return -1.
A subarray is a contiguous sequence of elements within an array.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [3,9,2,1,7], k = 3
Output: 7
Explanation:

1 appears in 2 subarrays of size 3: [9, 2, 1] and [2, 1, 7].
2 appears in 3 subarrays of size 3: [3, 9, 2], [9, 2, 1], [2, 1, 7].
3 appears in 1 subarray of size 3: [3, 9, 2].
7 appears in 1 subarray of size 3: [2, 1, 7].
9 appears in 2 subarrays of size 3: [3, 9, 2], and [9, 2, 1].

We return 7 since it is the largest integer that appears in exactly one subarray of size k.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [3,9,7,2,1,7], k = 4
Output: 3
Explanation:

1 appears in 2 subarrays of size 4: [9, 7, 2, 1], [7, 2, 1, 7].
2 appears in 3 subarrays of size 4: [3, 9, 7, 2], [9, 7, 2, 1], [7, 2, 1, 7].
3 appears in 1 subarray of size 4: [3, 9, 7, 2].
7 appears in 3 subarrays of size 4: [3, 9, 7, 2], [9, 7, 2, 1], [7, 2, 1, 7].
9 appears in 2 subarrays of size 4: [3, 9, 7, 2], [9, 7, 2, 1].

We return 3 since it is the largest and only integer that appears in exactly one subarray of size k.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [0,0], k = 1
Output: -1
Explanation:
There is no integer that appears in only one subarray of size 1.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 50
0 <= nums[i] <= 50
1 <= k <= nums.length

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def largestInteger(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's craft final solution:

```python
from typing import List
from collections import Counter

class Solution:
    def largestInteger(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        cnt = Counter()
        for i in range(n - k + 1):
            for x in set(nums[i:i+k]):
                cnt[x] += 1
        ans = -1
        for x, c in cnt.items():
            if c == 1 and x > ans:
                ans = x
        return ans
```

Let's maybe write a slightly more efficient version without slice to avoid memory? Not necessary. But if we want to be neat:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def largestInteger(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        window_count = {}
        
        for start in range(n - k + 1):
            seen = set()
            for i in range(start, start + k):
                seen.add(nums[i])
            for x in seen:
                window_count[x] = window_count.get(x, 0) + 1
        
        ans = -1
        for x, c in window_count.items():
            if c == 1:
                ans = max(ans, x)
        return ans
```

This avoids slice and Counter import. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def largestInteger(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        cnt = {}
        
        for start in range(n - k + 1):
            seen = set()
            for i in range(start, start + k):
                seen.add(nums[i])
            for x in seen:
                cnt[x] = cnt.get(x, 0) + 1
        
        ans = -1
        for x, c in cnt.items():
            if c == 1 and x > ans:
                ans = x
        return ans
```

This is final.

Let's maybe think if there is any issue with "nums = [1,2,3,4,5,6], k=1", if value
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def largestInteger(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        cnt = {}

        for start in range(n - k + 1):
            seen = set()
            for i in range(start, start + k):
                seen.add(nums[i])
            for x in seen:
                cnt[x] = cnt.get(x, 0) + 1

        ans = -1
        for x, c in cnt.items():
            if c == 1 and x > ans:
                ans = x

        return ans
```
